Long form, for my newsletter, The Polymath

A first-timer's guide to a big trade show, brain edition

What the studies found about noise, decisions, walking, sleep and small talk on a long show day

The morning walk in: visitors head along the Las Vegas Convention Center during CES in January 2010, the same building where MJBizCon 2026 takes place. For a first-timer, the long approach to the doors is the start of a long day. Photo: BenGrantham, Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

At ten in the morning on Wednesday, December 2, the expo hall doors open at the Las Vegas Convention Center, and MJBizCon's first full exhibition day begins. The organiser lists the floor as open until 5 p.m. that day and the next, and until 3 p.m. on Friday, December 4, with a promise of "800+ leading exhibitors" (MJBizCon, 2026). A first-time visitor usually arrives at a day like that with a badge and a folded floor map, planning to see everything. By mid-afternoon the plan has usually changed. The halls are louder than expected, and a simple decision about which booth to visit next feels oddly heavy.

This guide was written before MJBizCon 2026, so it uses no photographs from this year's event. It looks at the demands a long show day places on the brain, from background conversation and repeated decisions to walking, travel and sociable talk, and it asks what the research actually found about each one. Each finding comes with a plain note on how sure the science is. None of these studies took place on an exhibition floor, so the habits at the end are a reading of neighbouring evidence to try on the show floor. Hours can change, so check the organiser's site before you travel.

The talk you are not listening to

In a German laboratory, 22 adults recalled nine digits while trying to ignore spoken sentences. The clearer the ignored speech (2, 8 or 32 sound channels), the fewer digits they recalled in order. Cropped from Fig. 2 of Wöstmann and Obleser (2016), Frontiers in Human Neuroscience, CC BY 4.0.

The background roar of an exhibition floor is mostly people talking. Psychologists have studied this kind of sound since the 1970s under the label "irrelevant speech" (Wöstmann & Obleser, 2016). In a typical experiment, participants memorise a short list while recorded speech that they have been told to ignore plays through headphones.

In one recent version, 22 adults heard the digits 1 to 9 in random order and then rehearsed them silently while unrelated sentences played. Younger adults recalled 66 percent of the digits in the correct position when the ignored speech was blurred, and 58 percent when it was clear. Older adults declined from 52 to 45 percent. The authors concluded that "more acoustic detail, but not higher predictability, of task-irrelevant speech aggravated memory interference" (Wöstmann & Obleser, 2016). The pattern matches decades of earlier laboratory research, although this study was small and was run in a quiet room.

People also differ. A review concluded that people with greater working memory capacity, meaning the mental workspace for holding information in mind, are generally less distracted by sound and adapt to it faster (Sörqvist & Rönnberg, 2014). In a survey of 1,078 office workers in Italy, roughly two in three named lost concentration as the main effect of colleagues' conversations, although a questionnaire about feelings cannot show cause (Di Blasio et al., 2019).

Big choices early, and the argument over why

When dozens of laboratories ran the same willpower test at once, the effect that older studies reported nearly disappeared. Chart: Ask a Neuroscientist, from Carter and McCullough (2014), Dang et al. (2021) and Vohs et al. (2021) data.

Many first-time visitors put off their important meetings until the afternoon, after exploring the floor. Several field studies suggest that this order could matter. In 21,867 visits for coughs and colds to 204 primary care clinicians in Boston, the odds of an antibiotic prescription were about a quarter higher in the fourth hour of a clinic session than in the first (Linder et al., 2014). In another clinic study, 16 of 20 physicians ordered more tests in the first hour of the day than in the last, though only four of them showed a clear difference on their own (Trinh et al., 2021).

The most famous example comes from Israeli parole boards, where favourable rulings fell from about 65 percent to nearly zero across each session and recovered after a food break (Danziger et al., 2011). Critics soon pointed out that prisoners without lawyers were usually heard last within a session (Weinshall-Margel & Shapard, 2011). A later simulation showed that a judge who simply ends a session before starting a lengthy case could produce a similar curve (Glöckner, 2016).

The theory behind "decision fatigue" has suffered larger setbacks. It depends on ego depletion, the proposal that self-control is a resource that runs down like fuel. A 2010 review of 198 published experiments estimated a medium effect, but a reanalysis found strong signs that small studies with positive results had been favoured in print (Carter & McCullough, 2014). Two later projects ran an identical procedure in many laboratories at once. A 12-lab study found a small effect (Dang et al., 2021), while a 36-lab study with over 3,500 participants found almost none and concluded that depletion is less dependable than previously assumed (Vohs et al., 2021). In a preregistered analysis of 231,076 judgments by 174 Swedish telephone triage nurses, the data favoured no fatigue effect at all (Andersson et al., 2025).

Time of day may still influence decisions, but nobody has shown why, and the most carefully designed tests point toward a small effect or none. Scheduling important meetings early is therefore a cheap precaution, though no study has tested it at a trade show.

Walk between meetings, stand still to read

Twelve young adults walked a 10 metre laboratory walkway with and without side tasks. The hand task slowed them most. Cropped from Fig. 1 of Beurskens et al. (2016), Neural Plasticity, CC BY 4.0.

Walking is the one demand of the day that seems to help attention, and the evidence for it is fairly broad. A meta-analysis of 50 studies with 2,366 participants found a small benefit of a single session of aerobic exercise on interference control, the skill of responding to the right thing while ignoring a distraction (Oberste et al., 2019). Testing generally happened within about an hour of exercise. For light exercise, the closest equivalent to a stroll between halls, the seven available comparisons pointed toward a benefit, but the range of plausible values included no effect. An analysis of pooled individual data found that participants who performed worse before exercising gained the most, which may explain why some experiments detect nothing (Ishihara et al., 2021).

Walking while doing something else is a different matter. In the study pictured above, a listening task slowed walking by 2.9 percent, whereas a task performed with the hands slowed it by 15.1 percent and made steps less regular (Beurskens et al., 2016). Among 26 healthy adults, reading or typing on a phone made people walk more slowly and drift further from a straight line (Schabrun et al., 2014). A 2023 meta-analysis of 25 studies reports the same slowdown with texting, according to its abstract, the only part publicly available (Bruyneel et al., 2023). The practical reading is to use the walk to reset, and to step aside against a wall whenever you need to read the map or answer a message.

Sleep on the road

Across 24 studies, coffee taken closer to bedtime went with more lost sleep; the model's cut-off for a cup of coffee fell in the early afternoon for a 10 p.m. bedtime. Cropped from Fig. 3b of Gardiner et al. (2023), Sleep Medicine Reviews, CC BY 4.0 (panel title cropped).

Your first night in a strange hotel may well be your worst. Sleep researchers have long recorded a "first-night effect" in the laboratory. A meta-analysis of 53 studies found that people took longer to fall asleep and slept less on the first recorded night than on the second, with the smallest effect among people in their twenties, according to its abstract (Ding et al., 2022). In small experiments with about a dozen young adults each, one hemisphere of the brain slept more lightly on the first night and responded more strongly to unusual sounds, like a night watchman (Tamaki et al., 2016). The effect reappeared even when laboratory nights were a week apart (Wick et al., 2024).

Time zones add to the problem. Las Vegas keeps Pacific time, three hours behind the East Coast (15 U.S.C. § 261), and jet lag is usually described as beginning after flights across three or more zones (Roach & Sargent, 2019). In 1.5 million nights of sleep-ring data from 57,240 travellers, sleep loss was greater after eastward trips, and sleep timing had not fully returned to normal fifteen days afterwards (Willoughby et al., 2025). For most East Coast visitors, the eastward journey is the flight home.

Coffee timing matters as well. A meta-analysis of 24 studies found that caffeine reduced total sleep by about 45 minutes on average, and its model put the cut-off for a cup of coffee about nine hours before bedtime (Gardiner et al., 2023). In a later trial in 23 young men, a high amount taken within 12 hours of bedtime disrupted measured sleep, yet participants often failed to notice the damage (Gardiner et al., 2025). That trial tested only men, so the finding is still early.

Why a good conversation can tire you later

Among 74 Helsinki students texted four times a day, an extra sociable hour went with feeling more tired at the next check-in, and not in the moment. Chart: Ask a Neuroscientist, from Leikas (2020) data.

The final cost is the one people discuss least. In an experience-sampling study, 74 students at the University of Helsinki answered a short questionnaire four times a day for four days. Whenever a student had been more sociable than usual during the previous hour, they reported more tiredness two to three hours later, even though sociability went with a better mood at the time (Leikas, 2020). The study repeated an earlier, smaller one by the same research team, and it found no sign that only introverts paid this cost, although the sample was too small to test personality differences properly (Leikas, 2020).

A one-week randomised trial extended the idea. Participants told to behave more outgoingly felt happier and more authentic on average, while the more introverted among them reported extra tiredness and negative mood when looking back on the week (Jacques-Hamilton et al., 2019). That account comes from the authors' accepted manuscript; a correction to the paper appeared in 2026 and is not openly available. Two studies agree on a delayed dip in energy, and one trial suggests it may affect introverts most, which is still a theory being tested.

Back on the floor

The Las Vegas Convention Center from Paradise Road during CES in January 2009, with the monorail overhead. The building's size is the first thing a first-timer underestimates. Photo: BenGrantham, Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

The venue's own brochure describes over 2.5 million square feet of exhibit space spread across its North, Central, South and West halls, and it directs visitors to the monorail and an underground loop for crossing the campus (Las Vegas Convention and Visitors Authority [LVCVA], 2024). At that scale, attention has to last through a whole day of long distances and constant noise.

Taken together, the evidence supports a modest plan. Book the meetings that matter early, since it costs nothing even while the reason is disputed. Walk between halls, and stand still whenever you read. Allow quiet minutes after a series of conversations, since the tiredness may arrive hours later. Keep the strong coffee for mornings, and expect both the first night and the flight home to disturb your sleep.

Carry a water bottle as well, with modest expectations. A review found little evidence that mild dehydration disrupts thinking in young adults under ordinary conditions (Benton, 2011), while a trial in which 12 young men went 36 hours without water found reduced vigour and memory that improved after drinking (Zhang et al., 2019). An exhibition day is far from that extreme. The most useful instrument may be your own attention: notice when your concentration fades, and which habit seemed to help.

Faces in things: why your brain sees faces in sockets, cars and toast

Also that week: wider neuroscience Post ID PDFT01

In this post: Instagram carousel (6 slides), caption, pinned first comment, alt text, 3 Stories with stickers, single post, animated Reel, slideshow Reel, caption files, video alt text · Facebook Page same carousel and Reel · Bluesky post, sources reply · TikTok post · YouTube Shorts cover, title, description · Pinterest pin, title, description · LinkedIn page post, my profile (kept, not posted) · Threads and X paused, kept for later · Filmed video spoken script · Reshares day 14, day 28 · On the web sources page, scroll page, interactive, long form

Instagram

Carousel, slide by slide

Caption

A socket that looks surprised, a car that grins: seeing faces in things is called face pareidolia.
And your brain's face system takes part. Written for the Psychedelics Design Conference 2026 series on the neuroscience of psychedelic design.

Fast: in 7 adults, face-like objects drew a face-area response at about 165 milliseconds, like real faces (Hadjikhani et al., 2009). A small study; later work agrees.

Then corrected: in 22 people, illusory faces were first represented as face-like and, within about 250 milliseconds, as ordinary objects. The authors describe a face detector tuned for sensitivity over selectivity (Wardle et al., 2020). Two kinds of brain scan agree.

Expectation matters: told that noise hid faces, 20 adults saw one 34% of the time (Liu et al., 2014). Rhesus monkeys look longer at illusory faces (Taubert et al., 2017), yet in a later study their face cells barely responded to them (Koyano et al., 2025). One account says perception is the brain's best guess (Corlett et al., 2019). Scientists still disagree on the details.

Psychedelics and pareidolia: no controlled study found yet.

Sources: Wardle et al. (2020), Nature Communications; Hadjikhani et al. (2009), NeuroReport; Liu et al. (2014), Cortex; Taubert et al. (2017), Current Biology; Koyano et al. (2025), Progress in Neurobiology; Corlett et al. (2019), Trends in Cognitive Sciences.

Images: photos by Ariane Middel, Mark Turnauckas, somegeekintn, Milestoned, Tom Hentoff, Paul L Dineen and Ruth and Dave on Flickr (CC BY 2.0); Topher, WordPress Photos (CC0); Wardle SG et al. (CC BY); Martin Predavec, iNaturalist observation 323931295 (CC0); citi2024, iNaturalist observation 395535340 (CC0); NASA (public domain); charts and a number card made for this post from the cited studies.

What is the funniest face you have spotted in an everyday object?

Alt text, per slide (read aloud by screen readers)
1. Photograph of a North American wall socket whose two slots and round ground hole look like wide eyes and an open mouth.
2. Axis drawn to scale from 0 to 300 milliseconds, with a mark at 130 for people and at 165 for objects too.
3. Chart titled "Told apart from plain objects?": brain-scan (MEG) accuracy over the first 500 ms in 22 people. Real faces reach 65.5% at 160 ms and 65.3% at 250 ms; face-like objects reach 56.6% at 160 ms and fall to 52.7% by 250 ms, near the 50% chance line.
4. Published figure: the study's own photographs of illusory faces in objects (green peppers, a coffee cup, a washing machine, a propeller plane, an onion, a mixer) above matched objects without a face.
5. Photograph of a rhesus macaque sitting on a post by a river, looking toward the camera.
6. Two NASA images of the same Martian mesa: the 1976 Viking frame, where shadows look like eyes and a mouth, and the 2001 high-resolution view, an ordinary eroded hill.

Single image post

Your brain treats a face-like socket as a face for a moment, then sorts it back into objects within about 250 milliseconds (Wardle et al., 2020). Two kinds of brain scan agree.

Ask a Neuroscientist page

Faces in things: why your brain sees faces in sockets, cars and toast
This Ask a Neuroscientist post comes from RN Collins, Ask a Neuroscientist's resident neuroscientist. Series 12 of "the neuroscience of psychedelic design", written for the Psychedelics Design Conference 2026, with a find-the-face game on the post's page.
Takeaway: the face system responds to face-like objects within about a sixth of a second and re-files them as objects soon after (Hadjikhani et al., 2009; Wardle et al., 2020).
#AskANeuro
I'm taking on clients for storytelling strategy and content production and operations. DMs are open.
My own LinkedIn (written, not posted for now)
I'm RN Collins, staff writer and resident neuroscientist at Fat Nugs Magazine.
For designers, researchers and science communicators: face pareidolia has unusually clean timing data. In MEG (n = 7), face-like objects evoked a fusiform response at about 165 ms, similar to faces (Hadjikhani et al., 2009). With 96 images, fMRI and MEG (n = 22), illusory faces were first represented closer to human faces than matched objects, then like objects within about 250 ms, consistent with a detector that privileges sensitivity over selectivity (Wardle et al., 2020). Expectation alone produced face reports in 34% of pure-noise images (Liu et al., 2014, n = 20), and rhesus monkeys looked longer at such objects (Taubert et al., 2017, n = 5), though in a later study their face-patch neurons barely responded (Koyano et al., 2025).
Takeaway: a face-like arrangement draws the face system early, then the object is recognised for what it is.
#AskANeuro
I'm taking on clients for storytelling strategy and content production and operations. DMs are open.

Threads post

A socket that looks surprised: your brain agrees, briefly. Face-like objects reach the brain's face area in about 165 milliseconds (Hadjikhani et al., 2009), and within about 250 milliseconds the brain files them back as objects (Wardle et al., 2020). Monkeys look longer at them too, though their face cells barely respond (Taubert et al., 2017; Koyano et al., 2025). What object in your home has a face? #AskANeuro

Threads first reply

Sources pageScroll pageInteractive: Find the face

The sources page lists every study and image behind the post and opens to the public on Fri, Dec 4, 2026; this is its preview. The scroll page is the post as one web page on the Ask a Neuroscientist site.

Long form, for my newsletter, The Polymath

Faces in things: why your brain sees faces in sockets, cars and toast

A green pepper, a coffee cup and a fifth of a second: what brain recordings and monkeys reveal about the faces we find everywhere.

A wall outlet that seems to gasp, photographed in December 2010. Two slots and a hole are enough for most people to see a startled face, a mistake the visual brain makes every day. Photo: jnyemb, "Shocked Face [1]", Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

In a magnetically shielded room in Sydney, Australia, 22 volunteers lay on their backs with their heads inside a scanner. Photographs of people and of ordinary things, such as a green pepper or a cup of coffee, flashed above them for a fifth of a second each. A few of the objects seemed to look back, because the pepper had two eye holes above a gaping mouth and the coffee foam had settled into a grin (Wardle et al., 2020).

Seeing a face in something that has none is called face pareidolia. Susan Wardle and colleagues at the US National Institute of Mental Health ran the Sydney study to catch the illusion in the act. About 200 papers in Europe PMC mention pareidolia in their title or abstract as of November 2026. This piece draws on the ones that test how the illusion works, and says for each finding how sure the science is.

This article is part of Ask a Neuroscientist's series for Psychedelics Design 2026, whose main question here, whether psychedelic states change how often people see faces, no one has tested.

A test set of 96 photographs

The study's own pictures. Top row: objects with an illusory face. Bottom row: matched objects without one. Every illusory face had a twin like this, so any difference in the brain came from the face alone. Wardle et al. (2020), Nature Communications, cropped from Fig. 1a (human-face row removed), CC BY 4.0.

The team gathered 96 photographs: 32 objects with an illusory face, 32 human faces, and 32 matched objects with no face, so a pepper with a face was paired with a plain pepper. Twenty online raters scored each image from 0 to 10 for how easily they saw a face. Human faces averaged 9.96, illusory faces 6.27, and their twins 0.70 (Wardle et al., 2020).

Functional MRI, or fMRI, in 16 people showed where the brain responded. Magnetoencephalography, or MEG, which reads the brain's tiny magnetic signals, showed when, to the millisecond (Wardle et al., 2020).

A sixth of a second

Recorded from inside the brain. In 44 people with epilepsy who had electrodes implanted for their care, 39 face-selective sites in the fusiform gyrus began responding to face-like objects (blue) at 136 ms, ten milliseconds after human faces (red). Cerrahoğlu et al. (2025), Imaging Neuroscience, Fig. 6, CC BY 4.0.

Nouchine Hadjikhani and colleagues at Massachusetts General Hospital found that the fusiform face area, on the underside of the brain, responded to face-like objects at about 165 milliseconds, much as it did to real faces, and not to plain objects. That 2009 study rested on seven brains, because two of nine volunteers moved too much (Hadjikhani et al., 2009). In Sydney, a program reading only the MEG signal told illusory faces from their twins best at about 160 milliseconds. Only face-selective regions could tell the two apart (Wardle et al., 2020).

Scalp recordings agree. In 26 students, a brain wave called the N170, which is larger for faces, was also larger for face-like objects than for plain ones (Proverbio & Galli, 2016).

The most direct test came in 2025, when Begüm Cerrahoğlu, Bruno Rossion and colleagues in Nancy, France, recorded from electrodes inside the brains of 44 people being assessed for epilepsy surgery. Of the recording sites that responded to face-like objects, 89 percent also responded to real faces. The signal was about a quarter as strong, and it started at almost the same moment (Cerrahoğlu et al., 2025). One MEG study using a different set of images disagrees: the activity that separated images seen as faces from the same images turned upside down came at later stages (Romagnano et al., 2024). Most of the evidence, from four methods, points to a fast first step.

Face first, object a moment later

The face that fades. The blue line shows how well the MEG signal from 22 people separated illusory faces from their matched objects: a sharp peak near 160 ms, then a quick slide. Real faces (red) stay distinct far longer. Wardle et al. (2020), Nature Communications, cropped from Fig. 5a, CC BY 4.0.

The Sydney data held a surprise: the face signal for objects was brief. At 160 milliseconds the brain grouped illusory faces with real faces, and by about 250 milliseconds it grouped them with ordinary objects (Wardle et al., 2020).

They describe a face detector that is "broadly-tuned" and "privileges sensitivity over selectivity": it would sooner raise a false alarm than miss a face (Wardle et al., 2020). The electrode study adds a twist. Inside the fusiform gyrus, the response to face-like objects lasted almost as long as the response to faces (Cerrahoğlu et al., 2025). The two studies measured different things, so how quickly the brain files the face away is still an open question.

Who sees the faces

"Happy Outlets in China," photographed in December 2013. People read moods, ages and even genders into faces like these. Photo: Edward Stojakovic (akasped), Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

Object faces seem to have moods. Students in Sydney rated 40 of them from angry to happy with consistent results, and each face's mood was pulled toward the one seen just before, as with human faces (Alais et al., 2021). Across experiments with 3,815 adults, people also gave illusory faces an age and a gender, and saw them as male about four times as often as female (Wardle et al., 2022). In 412 people aged 4 to 80, children showed the same male bias (Wardle et al., 2023).

A study titled "Women are better at seeing faces where there are none" found women's brain waves treated face-like objects more like faces (Proverbio & Galli, 2016). Later work is mixed: one study found no gender difference for upright images (Pavlova et al., 2020), and another found women simply set a looser bar for saying "face" (Wang & Yang, 2025). In 379 women tested online, those who had recently given birth saw faces more easily than those who were pregnant, which the authors call preliminary (Taubert et al., 2023).

The habit starts early. Babies of 10 and 12 months, but not 8 months, looked at the "mouth" of a simple blob face when a sound played (Kato & Mugitani, 2015). Some findings have not held up. Two earlier search studies found that illusory faces stand out in a crowd of objects, one against matched objects and a later one only against varied objects. A 2025 team then found that neither real nor illusory faces pulled attention away from another task in its experiments, and called its paper a failure to replicate (Miti et al., 2025).

What monkeys see

A wild rhesus macaque in Longhu Mountain Natural Reserve, Guangxi, China, November 3, 2015. Rhesus monkeys are the species in most animal tests of the illusion, and they give mixed answers. Photo: Donald Hobern, Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

Jessica Taubert and colleagues tracked the eyes of five rhesus monkeys and found they looked longer at objects with faces than at matched objects, gazing at the illusory "eyes" and "mouth." The team proposed a "broadly-tuned face detection mechanism that we share with other species" (Taubert et al., 2017). Brain scans of awake macaques later showed some face areas and the amygdala, a small structure linked to faces and emotion, responding more to illusory faces (Taubert et al., 2022).

Newer animal work, summed up in the 2025 electrode paper, suggests the illusion may be human specific (Cerrahoğlu et al., 2025). Face cells in monkeys responded to the "eye" parts more than to the whole arrangement that people use (Sharma et al., 2024), and in six monkeys the extra response to pareidolia objects was under 4 percent of the response to real faces (Koyano et al., 2025). Chimpanzees gave little sign of matching a face-like object to a face (Tomonaga & Kawakami, 2022). Whether other primates see the illusion is unsettled.

Seeing what you expect

The "Face on Mars," photographed by NASA's Viking 1 orbiter on July 25, 1976, while it searched for a landing site. The mesa is about 1.5 km across; the "eyes" and "nostrils" are shadows. Image: NASA/JPL, PIA01141, public domain.

Told that half of a set of pure-noise images held a hidden face, 20 young adults in China reported faces 34 percent of the time, and their right fusiform face area responded when they did (Liu et al., 2014). A later study using the same design found that only 4 of 9 people showed that response, and suggested the others may have just said what was expected (Zimmermann et al., 2019). Philip Corlett and colleagues describe perception as a best guess that mixes the senses with prior expectations, efficient but one that "can be prone to error" (Corlett et al., 2019).

Clinics use the illusion. People with dementia with Lewy bodies, where hallucinations are common, reported far more illusions on a pareidolia test than people with Alzheimer's disease (Uchiyama et al., 2012), and a 15-minute version flagged 81 percent of people with Lewy body dementia while correctly clearing 92 percent of those with Alzheimer's (Mamiya et al., 2016). Negative mood doubled these illusions in Lewy body dementia (Watanabe et al., 2018). A 2025 review of 44 studies found pareidolia tests useful there, though their link to hallucinations is unclear (Capogna et al., 2025). Schizophrenia gives mixed results. One study of 150 people found more pareidolia in schizophrenia (Abo Hamza et al., 2021), while the team that makes faces out of arranged food reports that people with schizophrenia see fewer faces in such images (Romagnano et al., 2025).

A 2025 review says pareidolia is "not emphasized in the psychedelic literature" beyond online anecdotes (Heller et al., 2025). A paper proposing a model of psychedelic effects calls it "consistently observed with psychedelic use," but the two studies it cites, on creativity and on psychiatric patients, did not test psychedelics (Safron et al., 2025; Bellemare-Pepin et al., 2022; Mavrogiorgou et al., 2021). The nearest data are one report of a person who kept seeing faces in trees after using LSD (Iaria et al., 2010), and four EEG studies in which psychedelics suppressed the N170; two used faces and two used outline shapes, and neither kind was an illusory face (Heller et al., 2025). No controlled study has measured face pareidolia during a psychedelic state.

What designers can borrow

A smart concept car on display, photographed January 14, 2012. Hadjikhani's team noted that head-on views of cars may read as faces. Photo: Barbara Eckstein, "Happy Smart Car," Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

A face in a product or logo reaches the face system in about a sixth of a second and is soon filed as an object again, so it catches the eye without confusing anyone (Hadjikhani et al., 2009; Wardle et al., 2020).

People shown a camera algorithm's false alarms for 20 milliseconds glanced at them as faces more often than at non-faces ('t Hart et al., 2011). A team at MIT built "Faces in Things," 5,000 web images of pareidolia, and found that a leading face detector caught few of them; training it on animal faces roughly doubled its hits (Hamilton et al., 2024). Only networks trained both to recognise faces and to sort objects used "eyes" the way people do (Gupta & Dobs, 2025). The MIT team also found that people and machines see the most faces in images of middle complexity, a useful target for designers.

To feel the effect, try the "find the face" game on this post's page. Most people spot the faces before they can say why.

Resources

  • Wardle et al. (2020), full text on PubMed Central: the main study in this piece, free to read, with every figure.
  • The study's 96 pictures on the Open Science Framework: the illusory faces and matched objects in full resolution, posted by the authors.
  • Cerrahoğlu et al. (2025), full text on PubMed Central: the electrode study that recorded face-like responses inside the brain.
  • Faces in Things dataset (MIT): 5,000 annotated pareidolia photos and models from Hamilton et al. (2024).
  • Wardle et al. (2022), full text on PubMed Central: the 3,815-person study on the mood, age and gender people read into illusory faces.
  • Capogna et al. (2025), systematic review: 44 studies on pareidolia and other minor visual experiences in Lewy body disease.
  • Heller et al. (2025), full text on PubMed Central: a review comparing psychedelic and Lewy body hallucinations, with research questions on pareidolia.
  • NASA Photojournal: the Viking "Face on Mars": the 1976 image and NASA's own caption explaining the shadows.
  • Lewy Body Dementia Association: information and support for families living with Lewy body dementia, where pareidolia tests are used.
  • National Institute of Mental Health, Laboratory of Brain and Cognition: the federal lab where much of this pareidolia research began.

Education, not medical advice.

Sat, Dec 5, 2026, 09:00 HST

How does your brain map a building?

Also that week: wider neuroscience Post ID PDS01

In this post: Instagram carousel (7 slides), caption, pinned first comment, alt text, 3 Stories with stickers, single post, animated Reel, slideshow Reel, caption files, video alt text · Facebook Page same carousel and Reel · Bluesky post, sources reply · TikTok post · YouTube Shorts cover, title, description · Pinterest pin, title, description · LinkedIn page post, my profile (kept, not posted) · Threads and X paused, kept for later · Filmed video spoken script · Reshares day 14, day 28 · On the web sources page, scroll page, long form

Instagram

Carousel, slide by slide

Caption

Your brain maps every building you walk through. Whether curved rooms suit that brain better is a more open question.

Textbook science: place cells and grid cells, found in rats in 1971 and 2005, form a positioning system in the brain. The work won the 2014 Nobel Prize. In 42 men (clear in 34) exploring a virtual arena, a grid-like brain signal appeared, and a steadier signal went with better spatial memory (Doeller et al., 2010). The signal has repeated in many active-navigation studies, though not in two where people were moved passively, one preregistered with 110 adults (Newton et al., 2024; Kransberg et al., 2026).

Many studies agree for shapes; for rooms, scientists still disagree: across 106 samples, people preferred curves overall (Hedges' g = 0.39). For images of interiors and facades the effect was about zero, and design experts showed none (Chuquichambi et al., 2022).

Still early: a 2025 review of 56 papers links memorable, easy-to-read places to wayfinding and asks for better measures (Maestre et al., 2025). For the Nov 6 to 7 talks on the architecture of ritual, that was the frontier.

Sources: Nobel Assembly at Karolinska Institutet (2014); Gobbo et al. (2022); Ulsaker-Janke et al. (2023), PNAS; Doeller, Barry & Burgess (2010), Nature; Newton et al. (2024), Alzheimer's & Dementia; Kransberg et al. (2026), Imaging Neuroscience; Chuquichambi et al. (2022), Annals of the New York Academy of Sciences; Maestre et al. (2025), Landscape and Urban Planning.

Images: John O'Keefe photos by Per Henning / NTNU (CC BY 2.0), from NTNU's Flickr; Ulsaker-Janke et al. (CC BY); Chuquichambi EG et al. (CC BY); Charts and number cards made for this post from the cited studies.

Pinned first comment

Would you feel more at home in a curved room or a square room?
Every source: rn-selected-work.vercel.app/sources/pds01
Alt text, per slide (read aloud by screen readers)
1. Photo: neuroscientist John O'Keefe, white-haired and bearded, lecturing in a linen jacket, with cell firing maps on the screen behind him.
2. Photo: John O'Keefe, who discovered place cells, standing in a lecture hall with a slide remote in his hand.
3. Published figure: 4 grid cell firing maps from rats, each with an autocorrelation map below showing spots in a hexagonal pattern.
4. Bar chart drawn to scale: of 42 men, 34 showed a clustered grid-like signal orientation, 8 did not.
5. Published funnel plot: each dot is one effect size from the curvature meta-analysis, with a vertical line at the overall effect.
6. Bar chart: curve preference by stimulus, from 0.56 for abstract shapes to -0.04 for interiors and facades.
7. Bar chart drawn to scale: 56 imageability papers by method, 38 mixed, 11 quantitative and 7 qualitative.

Hook on frame 1

Which Nobel-winning cells map rooms?

Slideshow Reel caption

Which Nobel-winning cells map rooms?

Your brain maps every building you walk through. Whether curved rooms suit that brain better is a more open question.

Textbook science: place cells and grid cells, found in rats in 1971 and 2005, form a positioning system in the brain. The work won the 2014 Nobel Prize. In 42 men (clear in 34) exploring a virtual arena, a grid-like brain signal appeared, and a steadier signal went with better spatial memory (Doeller et al., 2010). The signal has repeated in many active-navigation studies, though not in two where people were moved passively, one preregistered with 110 adults (Newton et al., 2024; Kransberg et al., 2026).

Images: John O'Keefe photos by Per Henning / NTNU (CC BY 2.0), from NTNU's Flickr; Ulsaker-Janke et al. (CC BY); Chuquichambi EG et al. (CC BY); Charts and number cards made for this post from the cited studies.

How does your brain map a building?
This Ask a Neuroscientist post comes from RN Collins, Ask a Neuroscientist's resident neuroscientist, for the Psychedelics Design 2026 series on designed spaces.
Takeaway: the brain's positioning system is well shown (Nobel Assembly, 2014; Doeller et al., 2010); the preference for curved rooms is still debated (Chuquichambi et al., 2022).

My own LinkedIn (written, not posted for now)
I'm RN Collins, staff writer and resident neuroscientist at Fat Nugs Magazine.
For architects, designers and environmental psychologists: three levels of evidence on space and the brain. Textbook science: place and grid cells form a positioning system (Nobel 2014), with a grid-like fMRI signal in 42 men (clear in 34) in a virtual arena (Doeller et al., 2010), repeated in active-navigation studies but not in two passive-navigation ones (Newton et al., 2024, n = 53; Kransberg et al., 2026, preregistered, n = 110). Solid overall, contested for rooms: curve preference g = 0.39 across 106 samples, g = -0.04 for interiors and facades, about zero for experts (Chuquichambi et al., 2022). Still early: imageability and wayfinding, 56 papers, measures not yet standard (Maestre et al., 2025).
Takeaway: borrow the mapping science with confidence and treat claims about room shape as open questions.
Sources: Nobel Assembly (2014); Doeller et al. (2010), Nature; Newton et al. (2024); Kransberg et al. (2026); Chuquichambi et al. (2022), Ann N Y Acad Sci; Maestre et al. (2025), Landsc Urban Plan.

On the web

Sources pageScroll page

The sources page lists every study and image behind the post and opens to the public on Sat, Dec 5, 2026; this is its preview. The scroll page is the post as one web page on the Ask a Neuroscientist site.

Long form, for my newsletter, The Polymath

How does your brain map a building?

The brain's map of space is one of the best-tested findings in neuroscience. What a building's shape does to that map is much less settled.

Houses climb a hillside in Valparaíso, Chile, in August 2012. On Nov 7, 2026, the psychedelic researcher Christopher Timmermann named the city as a place where "you can get lost," in a conversation about how buildings and cities shape the mind. Photo: talber, Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

Christopher Timmermann came to London from Chile for its psychedelic research, as he told the architect Michel Rojkind in a recorded conversation shown on Nov 7, the second day of the Psychedelics Design 2026 conference, in a session called The Architecture of Ritual. When the talk turned to cities, Timmermann reached for Valparaíso, a Chilean city where, he said, "you can get lost." He added: "It offers a sense of continuous novelty."

He also named a worry: "There's something about cities that they're built for prediction." Rojkind, an architect based in Mexico City, said people now want to get from A to B without getting lost, and his advice was to "forget about A to B" (Timmermann & Rojkind, 2026).

Inside that designer's question sits a brain question: what happens in your head when you learn a place, and can a building or a city change it? Neuroscience answers the first half well. The second half is far less settled, so this piece moves from the best-tested findings to the weakest.

A map made of cells

John O'Keefe of University College London, photographed by Norway's NTNU on Sept. 11, 2014, weeks before he shared the Nobel Prize. In 1971 he found the "place cells" that fire when a rat is in one spot. Photo: Per Henning/NTNU, Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

The psychologist Edward Tolman, who watched rats learn mazes, had proposed that a "cognitive map" forms in the brain. In 1971 John O'Keefe recorded single nerve cells in the hippocampus, a curved structure deep in the brain, while rats moved freely around a room. One type of cell was always active when the rat was at a certain place, and other cells took other places. O'Keefe showed that these place cells were building an inner map, and that the hippocampus holds a different map for each environment (Nobel Assembly at Karolinska Institutet, 2014).

People have place cells too. In three independent studies, electrodes placed in patients during hospital treatment picked up cells that fired at particular spots as the patients steered through a virtual town or arena (Herweg & Kahana, 2018).

A grid laid over the floor

Edvard Moser at the University of Pavia, Italy, on March 1, 2023, where he gave a lecture on how the brain computes space and time. With May-Britt Moser he discovered grid cells in 2005. Photo: Università di Pavia, Flickr, CC BY 2.0.

In 2005 May-Britt and Edvard Moser found a second pattern in the entorhinal cortex, a region wired to the hippocampus. Certain cells there were active each time the rat passed through several spots arranged in a hexagonal grid, like the points of a honeycomb spread across the floor. Each cell had its own pattern, and together these grid cells form a coordinate system. Other cells in the same region respond to the direction of the head or to the border of the room. The Nobel committee called the whole circuit an "inner GPS," and O'Keefe and the Mosers shared the 2014 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine "for their discoveries of cells that constitute a positioning system in the brain" (Nobel Assembly at Karolinska Institutet, 2014).

In rats, this is as sure as neuroscience gets. But the classic recordings were made in boxes and plain rooms, and none studied architecture.

The same grid in people

In a 2010 brain-scanning study in London, 34 of 42 men showed a clearly aligned grid-like signal in the entorhinal cortex while they explored a virtual arena. Chart: Ask a Neuroscientist, from Doeller, Barry & Burgess (2010) data.

A brain scanner cannot see a single cell, so Christian Doeller, Caswell Barry and Neil Burgess (2010) looked for a group signal. If neighbouring grid cells share an orientation, they reasoned, the scan's signal should peak six times as a person turns full circle. They scanned 42 men exploring a virtual grassy plain ringed by a cliff. The six-fold signal appeared, strongest in the right entorhinal cortex. Grid orientations clustered clearly in 34 of the 42 men, and men with a steadier signal remembered locations better (Doeller et al., 2010).

Joshua Jacobs and colleagues (2013) then recorded single cells in 14 people who had electrodes implanted for epilepsy treatment. While they played a wayfinding game on a laptop, 14% of the cells in their entorhinal cortex fired in a grid, though less neatly than in rodents. In two more patients, grid-like cells changed their spacing as the virtual room changed size and shape (Nadasdy et al., 2017).

Europe PMC lists more than 20 later studies that report the scan signal, most of them in people steering through virtual spaces (reviewed by Herweg & Kahana, 2018). The signal is also fragile. Two recent studies that moved people passively through a virtual room found no signal across the group, one of them a preregistered study of 110 adults (Newton et al., 2024; Kransberg et al., 2026). Computer models suggest it may come mainly from cells tuned to head direction (Bin Khalid et al., 2024), and critics warn against assuming that grid cells are simply how people find their way (Ekstrom et al., 2020).

All of this happened on screens until Matthias Stangl and colleagues (2021) recorded from electrodes implanted in five people for epilepsy treatment as they walked around a real room about 5.9 by 5.2 metres. Slow brain waves in the medial temporal lobe, home of the brain's maps, were stronger when a person was within 1.2 metres of a wall. The study is small and measured waves rather than cells, yet it shows walls registering in the human map.

What a city's streets teach

Chicago's street grid at about 1:32 a.m. on May 15, 2025, photographed from the International Space Station. In a study of 397,162 game players, Chicago was the example of a city with a highly regular street grid. Photo: NASA (iss073e0080182), public domain.

If cities are built for prediction, do they change the people who learn them? London's taxi drivers spend three to four years learning about 25,000 streets for an exam called the Knowledge. In 2000, Eleanor Maguire's team found that 16 licensed drivers had more grey matter at the back of the hippocampus than men who did not drive taxis, more so the longer they had driven (Maguire et al., 2000). A later study followed 79 trainees through their training. The 39 who qualified gained grey matter there, while those who failed and 31 men who never trained did not (Woollett & Maguire, 2011). In ordinary people the link fades: among 90 young adults, hippocampal size did not predict how well they learned the layout of a desktop virtual world (Weisberg et al., 2019).

The largest test comes from a mobile game. Antoine Coutrot, Hugo Spiers and colleagues measured how 397,162 people in 38 countries found their way in Sea Hero Quest, in which players steer a boat to checkpoints they saw on a map. In 49 people tested in London and Paris, game scores tracked wayfinding on real streets (Coutrot et al., 2019). People who grew up outside cities did better on average, after allowing for age, gender and education. The team scored each country's street layout with a measure called street network entropy, low for grid cities such as Chicago and high for older, organic cities such as Prague. Players raised in grid cities did best on regular game levels, and the rest did best on tangled ones (Coutrot et al., 2022).

How sure is this? The sample is huge, but the effect is small. In the accepted version of the paper, the gap linked to where people grew up was a Hedges' g of 0.09, a standard measure of how far apart two groups are, against 0.98 for the gap between players under 25 and over 55 (Coutrot et al., 2022). It is a correlation measured in a game, and it says nothing about buildings.

Do people prefer curves?

Each bar counts results from studies that asked people to rate curved against angular images. Most fall above zero, which means curves were preferred, but some fall below. Figure: Chuquichambi et al. (2022), Fig. 2, CC BY 4.0.

Design writing makes a second claim about space: people prefer curves to sharp corners. The modern version comes from Moshe Bar and Maital Neta, who found that people liked everyday objects with curved outlines more than sharp-angled versions. In their 2007 follow-up, 16 volunteers in a scanner saw each picture for 85 thousandths of a second. The sharp versions were liked less and drew more activity in the amygdala, which the authors linked to a sense of threat (Bar & Neta, 2007).

A meta-analysis by Erick Chuquichambi and colleagues (2022) pooled 61 studies, with 309 effect sizes and 11,023 people. On average, people preferred curved outlines, with a Hedges' g of 0.39 that the authors call "consistent with a medium effect size," and they found no sign that small studies had inflated it. Their summary is careful: the preference is "a reliable but not universal phenomenon" (Chuquichambi et al., 2022).

Rooms are a harder case

Split by what people looked at, the preference for curves was clear for shapes and objects and close to zero for interiors and facades. Experts in architecture, design and the arts showed none. Chart: Ask a Neuroscientist, from Chuquichambi et al. (2022) data.

The average hides the result that matters for buildings. By type of image, the preference was clear for abstract shapes (g = 0.56) and real objects (g = 0.42). For interiors and building facades it was close to zero and not statistically significant (g = -0.04, across 73 effect sizes from 11 studies). Experts in architecture, design and the arts showed no preference, though only six of the 61 studies recruited experts. The preference was also larger when images flashed by (g = 0.75) than when people could look at length (g = 0.32) (Chuquichambi et al., 2022).

The best-known room study shows why. Oshin Vartanian and colleagues (2013) scanned 18 people looking at 200 photographs of rooms. Curved rooms were judged beautiful more often and drew more activity in the anterior cingulate cortex, a region tied to feeling, yet they did not make people more likely to choose "enter" over "exit."

Other room studies point both ways. When 198 people rated photographs of living rooms, curved versions won by a small margin, and only among women and only for modern rooms, though curves lowered stress ratings for everyone (Tawil et al., 2022). The team's earlier virtual-reality test, one of the 11 pooled studies, in which 42 people walked through matched rooms, found almost no differences, and the two that held up favoured angular rooms (Tawil et al., 2021). People furnishing a virtual living room picked slightly more angular furniture (Pohlmann et al., 2025), and a 2025 test found no clear preference for curved tables or chairs (Chuquichambi et al., 2025). On the other side, 35 people reported better moods and had lower heart rates in a curved virtual room than in a rectangular one (Strachan-Regan & Baumann, 2024), and a 2026 study of 30 people, which we could read only as an abstract, reports strong preferences for curved interiors (Chuquichambi et al., 2026). Anyone who tells you that curved rooms calm the brain is going beyond this evidence.

Places that are easy to read

Under the folded steel canopy of the Cineteca Nacional in Mexico City, which Rojkind Arquitectos expanded in 2012 while reclaiming part of the site as public space. Photo: Jay Sterling Austin, Flickr, CC BY 2.0, Feb. 5, 2016.

Designers have long tried to name what makes a place easy to learn. In his 1960 book The Image of the City, Kevin Lynch called it imageability and named five elements that help: paths, edges, districts, nodes and landmarks (Maestre et al., 2025). A systematic review of 56 papers reports that such places promote wayfinding, but finds no standard way to measure imageability and no study yet linking it to brain scans (Maestre et al., 2025). Inside buildings, a review of 84 publications on indoor wayfinding found the most work on floor plans, landmarks, signs and maps (Jamshidi et al., 2020).

Rojkind's test for a good building is generosity. "If architects would design buildings with more generosity towards cities, we would have better cities," he said, pointing to an open base that people can pass through and shade for the sidewalk (Timmermann & Rojkind, 2026). His firm's 2012 expansion of the Cineteca Nacional, a Mexico City film complex, reclaimed part of the site as public space (Rojkind Arquitectos, n.d.). No study has measured what its canopy does to the people beneath it.

Timmermann's own field has barely touched this. The only recordings of place cells under a psychedelic that we found come from rats: on a familiar track, LSD made their place cells fire less and map the track less precisely (Domenico et al., 2021). We found no study of psychedelics and the human map. A designer can safely borrow the fact that the brain builds maps of space and that walls register in them. The streets people grow up on leave a small mark on how they find their way. Whether curves make a room better for the brain is still open, and the next tests belong in places like Valparaíso and buildings like Rojkind's.

Education, not medical advice.

Resources

  • The Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine 2014: the committee's plain-language account of place cells and grid cells, with the key papers listed.
  • Herweg and Kahana (2018), open access: a readable review of place cells, grid cells and brain waves recorded in people.
  • Doeller, Barry and Burgess (2010), full text on Europe PMC: the author manuscript of the first human grid-cell scan, free to read.
  • Kransberg et al. (2026), open access: a preregistered null result, with a frank discussion of why the human grid signal is hard to catch.
  • Stangl et al. (2021), full text on PubMed Central: brain recordings from people walking in a real room.
  • Woollett and Maguire (2011), open access: the four-year study of trainee London taxi drivers.
  • Coutrot et al. (2022), accepted manuscript: the street network study, free to read from the University of East Anglia.
  • Sea Hero Quest, Alzheimer's Research UK: the game behind the wayfinding data, and the research portal for scientists.
  • Chuquichambi et al. (2022), open access: the curvature meta-analysis.
  • The meta-analysis data on the Open Science Framework: the table of all 309 effect sizes, so anyone can check the room result.
  • Tawil et al. (2021), open access: the virtual-reality room study that found no preference for curves.
  • Maestre et al. (2025), full text on Europe PMC: the imageability review, with its table of 56 studies.
  • Jamshidi et al. (2020), open access: a review of research on finding your way inside buildings.
  • Rojkind Arquitectos: Cineteca Nacional: the architects' own project page, with plans and credits.
  • Psychedelics Design 2026: the conference where Timmermann and Rojkind spoke.
